#7062. 6th Circuit upholds jury win for Ohio State in former graduate student’s sexual harassment lawsuit

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While the plaintiff argued the district court incorrectly instructed the jury, the 6th Circuit said she’d failed to raise these objections at the proper time.

A former Ohio State University graduate student could not convince a federal appeals court to overturn a jury’s verdict in favor of the school in her sexual harassment lawsuit because she failed to show that her trial was unfairly prejudiced, according to a Tuesday decision.

The plaintiff in Huang v. The Ohio State University alleged that a faculty advisor subjected her to unwanted touching and that, when she resisted his advances, he retaliated by manipulating her candidacy exam for an engineering doctorate. She brought claims under Title VII of the 1964 Civil Rights Act as well as the 14th Amendment’s due process right to bodily integrity.

A district granted summary judgment for Ohio State on the plaintiff’s Title VII claims, holding that she was not an employee of the university within the law’s definition and therefore could not bring those claims. At trial, a jury held for the plaintiff’s advisor. She appealed to the 6th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals, which reversed and ordered a new trial.

At the second trial, the jury determined that the plaintiff was not an employee because Ohio State and the advisor did not have the “right to control the manner and means by which [the plaintiff] accomplished her work.” The jury also ruled in favor of the advisor on the plaintiff’s 14th Amendment claim.

The plaintiff again appealed to the 6th Circuit, alleging that the district court incorrectly instructed the jury on what facts were relevant to its determination of whether she was an employee. She also claimed that the court abused its discretion in excluding certain evidence and that it improperly prohibited her from requesting economic damages in her closing argument.

Each of these claims failed, the 6th Circuit held.

The plaintiff forfeited her challenge to the court’s jury instructions because she failed to raise it, despite the district court specifically asking whether the parties had objections or edits to its instructions. To grant a third trial on this point “would be unfair to the court and the defendants,” the 6th Circuit said.

In her evidentiary argument, the plaintiff claimed that the district court improperly prevented her from submitting portions of a slideshow containing the results of an Ohio State campus climate survey on sexual assault. She argued that the presentation would have made her allegations more plausible.

However, the plaintiff failed to include the relevant slides in her appeal, leaving the 6th Circuit unable to conclude whether they had sufficient weight to affect the jury’s verdict. The appeals court also could not determine whether the slides offered anything “above and beyond” other testimony in the case.

In all, the plaintiff failed to show that any of her allegations amounted to unfair prejudice warranting a new trial.

The case represents one in a handful of recent jury decisions to favor employers facing Title VII lawsuits. In March, a jury held for Haribo of America in a former employee’s discrimination and retaliation case, determining that the plaintiff’s dismissal was a mutually agreed-upon separation between the parties rather than retaliation.

And in a 2025 case, a jury sided with Levi Strauss in a lawsuit alleging that a former executive did not receive a promotion because of her pregnancy, gender and age.

#7061. More students are homeless as districts face enrollment and budget losses

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A report from SchoolHouse Connection shows 1 in 32 students were identified as homeless, the highest share on record.

Between the 2018-19 and 2024-25 school years, the portion of students experiencing homelessness increased from 2.7% to 3.1%, with the overall population of students experiencing homelessness increasing 12%. During that same period, overall public school enrollment fell by about 1.3 million students.

Some of the largest increases in the share of homeless students were seen in states where homeless student identification increased alongside drops in overall public school enrollment. Those states include West Virginia, Montana, Arkansas, Hawaii, Maine and Vermont.

West Virginia had the largest share of homeless students compared to its overall public school population at 1 in 16.

Districts in that state have also closed around 140 since 2011, according to the West Virginia Center on Budget and Policy. Thirteen school closures have been proposed or approved in just the 2026-27 school year, according to the center's last update in mid-October.

Overall, the state has lost 3.5% of its students since last school year and 17% over the past decade, according to an October count by the West Virginia Department of Education.

"The drop in enrollment raises two concerns for students experiencing homelessness: tighter school budgets, and children who may not appear in school records at all," said the SchoolHouse Connection report about the issue nationally.

The budget squeeze, drop in overall enrollment, and increase in students identified as homeless come on the heels of federal pandemic relief funds drawing to an end in September 2024. The American Rescue Plan's Homeless Children and Youth funds provided a historic $800 million to identify and serve students experiencing homelessness. The additional funding allowed over half of U.S. school districts to access Education for Homeless Children and Youth funds.

In 2025-26, the first full year without ARP-HCY funds, many schools no longer had the staff and resources that helped them find these students, said SchoolHouse Connection. The report noted that the lost funding is a potential reason most states identified fewer homeless students despite a national increase in that population.

In fiscal year 2026, Congress appropriated only $129 million for the EHCY program, which means about 1 in 5 districts receive dedicated funding for homeless students. The EHCY program was established under the McKinney-Vento Homeless Assistance Act in 1987.

However, regardless of whether these funds are provided to districts, all districts are required under the McKinney-Vento Homeless Assistance Act to serve homeless students and remove barriers to their education.

#7057. Alabama lawmaker eyes 3-year bachelor’s degrees for public colleges

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The forthcoming proposal would allow the state’s universities and community colleges to create reduced-credit programs in high-demand fields.

Orr framed his proposal as a way to help affordability and meet workforce demands.

“Alabama higher education institutions need to be more innovative and accommodating to meet the needs of students in the 21st century,” he said on social media Saturday.

The Commission on Colleges and Universities, the accreditor that oversees most public colleges in Alabama, has already paved the way for three-year degrees in the state. In March, it approved a formal pathway to allow for the creation of reduced-hour bachelor’s degrees with 90 to 100 required credits.

Traditional bachelor’s degrees typically require at least 120 credits.

“We want institutions to have the flexibility to design programs that help students reach their goals efficiently while ensuring that the degrees they earn continue to reflect the quality and credibility that accreditation represents,” Stephen Pruitt, the accreditor's president, said at the time.

Though much of the discussion around accelerated bachelor’s degrees is theoretical, the model has gained increasing popularity in the last few years.

In early 2023, no U.S. colleges offered three-year reduced-credit bachelor's degrees , according to the nonprofit Jobs for the Future. Since then, some 100 such programs have been announced or launched.

Some states have already approved such programs.

A 2025 Iowa law directed the state’s three public universities to create three-year bachelor's degrees as a means of addressing workforce demands and decreasing the cost of college attendance. The state board of regents signed off on two such program proposals this month, pending regulatory approval.

One is an online bachelor’s in applied professional studies at the University of North Iowa, expected to launch next fall.

Deirdre Heistad, the university’s interim associate provost for academic affairs, said in a Nov. 12. statement that the new program will serve adults with some college credit but no credentials, many of whom “work as educational support professionals, service coordinators or in other fields where a bachelor’s degree is increasingly expected at the next level.

At least two private institutions in Iowa, Coe College and Upper Iowa University, already offer an accelerated bachelor’s program.

And in September, the Oklahoma State Regents for Higher Education changed its policies to allow the creation of four new accelerated bachelor’s degrees at three public universities.

Other states are still weighing the concept.

This summer, the State Council of Higher Education for Virginia announced it would partner with Ohio higher ed officials to create a blueprint for 90-credit bachelor’s degrees. Both states have laws mandating that bachelor’s degrees from their public colleges require at least 120 credits.

#7056. Storage Platform Engineer at Supabase

Supabase runs millions of Postgres databases, and storage performance is now one of the biggest levers on how fast, reliable, and affordable they are. We're building a software-defined, NVMe-over-Fabrics storage platform underneath Supabase Postgres. Postgres stays unmodified, and the new layer delivers near-local-NVMe performance with the durability and flexibility of networked storage.

As the senior engineer driving storage, you'll own this effort end to end: the architecture and build-vs-buy decisions, integration into our compute platform, and operating the system in production.

Your first goal is raw Postgres I/O performance at scale. In the longer term, the layer you build will provide the block-level snapshots and copy-on-write clones behind database branching, fast restores, and instant resizes. Because it's software-defined, it will also travel with us as we expand beyond a single cloud provider into multi-cloud and colocated infrastructure.

This is a foundational, high-ownership role. You'll set the technical direction for storage at Supabase and help grow the team around it.

Lead the architecture of Supabase's NVMe-oF storage platform, including evaluating and deciding between adopting existing software-defined storage and building on SPDK

Integrate networked block storage into our Kubernetes-based compute platform through CSI drivers and custom controllers, covering provisioning, attach/detach, resizing, and migration

Make Postgres fast on networked storage by profiling and tuning the full I/O path, from fsync, WAL, and checkpoints through the kernel block layer, NVMe/TCP, and the network

Design for durability and availability, including replication or erasure coding, failure domains, failover, and data-integrity verification

Build I/O isolation and QoS so that noisy neighbors can't degrade other tenants

Set performance and reliability SLOs, and build the benchmarking and observability to hold the system to them

Design the layer for block-level snapshots and copy-on-write clones, laying the groundwork for database branching

Keep the platform portable across AWS, other clouds, and bare-metal/colo

Write design docs, mentor engineers, and work closely with the Compute, Branching, Postgres, and Multicloud teams

Participate in on-call rotations, incident response, and post-incident reviews

Resolve escalated support issues, while also identifying and resolving systemic issues across the platform

Have 7+ years of software engineering experience, including significant time building and operating storage systems in production

Have built or operated distributed or software-defined block storage (for example Ceph RBD, Lightbits, Simplyblock, Portworx, LINSTOR/DRBD, or an in-house system)

Have hands-on experience with NVMe/TCP or NVMe-oF, including targets, initiators, multipath, and failover behavior

Have a deep understanding of Linux storage internals: the block layer, io_uring, page cache, O_DIRECT and fsync semantics, filesystems, and device-mapper

Write strong systems code in Go, plus C, C++, or Rust

Measure performance rigorously with fio, pgbench, eBPF, and perf, and can explain latency down to its individual components

Understand durability tradeoffs between replication, erasure coding, and consistency under failure

Have led technical direction on a complex system: design docs, tradeoffs, and bringing others along

Have experience with Kubernetes controllers and/or CSI drivers

Have been on call for large production systems

Communicate clearly in writing and thrive in async, globally distributed teams

Experience implementing block-level snapshots, thin provisioning, or copy-on-write cloning

Postgres internals knowledge (WAL, buffer manager, checkpointer), or familiarity with OrioleDB

Experience with AWS networking and instance storage at scale (ENA, placement, bandwidth limits)

Experience running storage on bare metal or in colo

Contributions to open source storage projects or the Linux kernel storage stack

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7055. Increases in pre-K discipline rates alarm advocates

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Preschool enrollment grew just 12% between 2021-22 and 2023-24, but suspensions increased 67%, an analysis by two equity groups found.

The number of U.S. preschoolers receiving out-of-school suspensions increased 67% and the expulsion rate nearly doubled between the 2021-22 and 2023-24 school years, according to an analysis of federal data by EdTrust and AdvancED Equity.

The rise in discipline rates for the youngest students may, in part, be due to a growing preschool enrollment, said Maithreyi Gopalan, co-author of the report and co-lead of AdvancEd Equity. Enrollment in school district-led preschool increased from 1.4 million in 2021-22 to 1.5 million in 2023-24.

Enrollment could be inching up due to a rebound from sharp drops during the COVID-19 pandemic, as well as expansion of public preschool programs in recent years, said Gopalan, who is also an associate professor at the University of Oregon's College of Education.

But the increase in students disciplined outpaced the overall enrollment growth of preschoolers, Gopalan said. "It's kind of shocking, isn't it, when you're thinking about 3- to 5-year-olds. What are they getting suspended for or expelled from school?"

The analysis by EdTrust and AdvancED Equity of preschool discipline rates uses data from the U.S. Department of Education's Civil Rights Data Collection and does not include information about what types of incidents led to the discipline.

A recent report from The Children’s Equity Project at Arizona State University found that harsh punishments used in preschool programs can lead to negative, long-term impacts like lower academic achievement and reduced high school graduation rates.

The CRDC only includes statistics from school district-supported preschool programs, Gopalan said. That means preschool enrollment would be included for district-operated early childhood Head Start programs, but not for Head Start programs run by community grantees.

And while the CRDC data is critical for tracking national trends, it does not capture the whole picture of discipline activities throughout the early childhood education system, said Gopalan, adding she and other researchers are continuing to look at different data collections to "get a better sense of what's going on."

What the CRDC data does reveal is that, like K-12 exclusionary discipline data, disparities in preschool punishments exist. The 2023-24 CRDC data shows Black preschoolers made up 18% of the pre-K population but were 37% of students who received suspensions. Boys were 54% of preschool enrollment but accounted for 81% of those suspended. White preschoolers were also overrepresented with 44% of the pre-K population but 51% of those suspended or expelled, according to the EdTrust and AdvancED Equity report and Gopalan.

Gopalan also noted in a follow-up email that the CRDC is limited, as it only collects formal out-of-school suspensions and expulsions. She said there is no federal collection that tracks more informal preschool discipline activities like shortened days, repeated pick-up calls or when parents are counseled to withdraw their child from a preschool.

Social-emotional learning interventions can help prevent escalating and challenging student behaviors, Gopalan said. And teacher training and resources also can reduce educators' stress and increase their capacity to address difficult behaviors.

"I think we as a society need to ask, 'Do we want to suspend them, or do we want to provide additional supports for teachers to help manage this problem?'" Gopalan said.

#7052. Federal student lending caps are ‘blunt instrument’ to prevent defaults, researchers say

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A “richer set of criteria,” including program earnings and credit profiles, would better address that risk, according to a new paper from the Federal Reserve Bank of Philadelphia.

The Republican spending bill enacted in 2025 ended the Grad PLUS lending program and set hard lifetime caps on federal student lending at $100,000 for graduate students and $200,000 for professional students — a classification being disputed in court.

Republicans aimed to reduce student debt loads through the law. When it passed, Rep. Tim Walberg, the Republican chair of the House Education and Workforce Committee, said the loan overhaul would help ensure “students don’t borrow excessive debt they can never repay.”

To examine the potential impact of the caps, the Philadelphia Fed research looked at three-year default rates among borrowers who took out federal graduate student loans and entered repayment between 2011 and 2016.

The overall default rate for those borrowing over the new limits — in inflation-adjusted dollars pegged to 2025 — of 6.5% for graduate students compares to a 3.3% rate for those borrowing below the limits. Master’s students drove up that rate, as they defaulted 7.2% of the time when borrowing above loan cap levels.

But the size of students’ federal loans wasn’t the only factor that predicted defaults.

Another was borrowers’ credit risk profiles. Three-year default rates for all graduate borrowers declined as their credit scores improved, the Philadelphia Fed found.

Borrowers with credit scores between 300 and 579 — deemed “deep subprime” — defaulted at a 12.4% rate. That rate declined until it reached just half a percent for those with scores between 770 to 850, the highest credit scores.

In every credit score bracket, students who borrowed above the new limits defaulted more often than those who borrowed less than the capped levels.

But those credit profiles don’t tell the full story, the researchers noted. The earnings potential of students’ fields of study also played a major role in defaults. Sub-prime and near-prime borrowers who studied in the highest-earning programs defaulted less frequently than less-risky prime borrowers in lower-earning programs.

“Loan limits are a far coarser signal of loan performance than either creditworthiness or program characteristics,” the researchers wrote.

But under the new caps, riskier student borrowers could be locked out of higher-earning programs by a private lending market that is expected to take over a larger share of student lending.

Following the Democratic sweep of the midterm elections, the federal loan caps could come under political pressure. Rep. Bobby Scott, the ranking Democratic member of the House education committee, has blasted the federal loan caps.

In March, for example, he and other Democrats on the committee said that the lending structure will “further restrict higher education access, particularly for low- and middle-income borrowers” and “destabilizes our higher education system and harms students.”

However, any changes to the new lending system in the near term would require sign-off by President Donald Trump or enough Republicans joining with Democrats to overturn a veto.

#7050. Coaching key to Alabama’s literacy success, report finds

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The state’s approach to placing a dedicated literacy coach in every school influenced the success seen later in Mississippi, a Fordham report says.

“The story is so fascinating to me because it’s very rare in education that you can draw a straight line from policy to achievement scores improvement. And in this case, people across the state seem to agree on what works—because they spent the early 2000s doing it," said Loewus.

Beginning in 2012, Alabama, however, reduced funding while expanding the coaching cadre to K-12 and across other subjects like math. By 2019, Alabama ranked last in the nation in 4th grade reading.

In the meantime, Mississippi had noticed Alabama’s early success and replicated the model. “They flip-flopped,” Loewus explained. And by 2019, Alabama looked at Mississippi and passed comprehensive legislation that more or less reinstituted its reading initiative, with funding for 750 school-based coaches, 100 regional coaches, and 10 staff at the state Department of Education to monitor the entire process, she said.

The Alabama legislation also protects coaches’ time: they cannot be pulled into substitute teaching when someone calls in sick, proctor tests, or help with recess. “That is so unusual,” Loewus said. “In other schools, coaches are the first ones to be pulled. They don’t have their own class.”

The state superintendent Eric Mackey requires district superintendents to sign a memorandum of understanding before they receive their annual allocation, and “when principals start to pull coaches for other things, he gets a phone call,” she added.

Overall, the legislation in Mississippi is mostly similar, Loewus said. The program there was initially funded by a now-shuttered philanthropic group called the Barksdale Reading Institute, but the state has since taken over the budget. “It’s really top down in both places,” she said. “It’s key having really resolute state leadership, champions at the top who are not letting this go.”

Loewus emphasizes that the coaching alone won’t achieve desired results unless the coaches are centrally trained in research-based reading instruction.

“The fact that everyone is getting the same training, and it’s centralized, and based on research, is really important. Protecting coaches’ time is really important. Having steady funding is important," Loewus added. "Having the structure of coaches having coaches is incredibly important.”

About 44 states and Washington, D.C., have passed science of reading policies, according to a 2025 update from Whiteboard Advisors.

#7049. Software Engineer - Postgres Tooling at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, Postgres Tooling to join our CLI team and help build the open-source tools developers use to change their Postgres schemas safely. You’ll join as a co-maintainer of pg-delta, our schema diff and migration engine, and grow into leading it.

Given two databases, or a database and a folder of SQL files describing the schema you want, pg-delta works out the differences and generates the migration to get from one to the other. It powers supabase db diff, db pull, and declarative schemas, and it runs underneath Supabase’s platform, branching, and AI agent workflows. Every migration it generates is verified on a throwaway clone before it’s trusted, and it’s tested against 331 scenarios on PostgreSQL 14–18. We’re now working toward a stable, standalone 1.0 release for any Postgres developer, on Supabase or not.

This is a correctness-first role. The SQL this tool generates runs against production databases, so privilege safety and avoiding data loss are non-negotiable. It’s a great fit for someone who loves Postgres at the catalog level, enjoys compiler-style problems, and thrives in async, fast-paced environments.

Co-maintain pg-delta: review and merge changes, including PRs written by AI agents, and grow into its lead maintainer.

Own correctness: triage bug reports from GitHub and support, fix diff and dependency-ordering bugs, and extend test coverage across Postgres versions.

Make sure generated migrations are privilege-safe and never silently lose data, by detecting destructive changes and rewriting them into safe equivalents where possible.

Help take pg-delta to a stable 1.0: a public API with a semver contract, documentation, and broader coverage of Postgres extensions.

Work with the open-source community: triage issues, review outside contributions, and say no gracefully when a request doesn’t fit.

Work with the teams across Supabase that build on pg-delta, so migrations are generated and run the same way everywhere.

Write the design docs and technical strategy that set the tool’s direction, working async across teams.

Share the CLI team’s on-call rotation. Weekdays are covered follow-the-sun, and because we ship packaged software that users can roll back, pages are rare.

Have 8+ years of experience as a software engineer, including deep, hands-on work with PostgreSQL.

Know Postgres internals at the catalog level: pg_catalog, pg_depend, object ownership, grants and default privileges, RLS policies, and extension membership.

Understand DDL and migration semantics: which changes take locks, which can’t run inside a transaction, how dependencies determine ordering, and how to spot a destructive change.

Treat a migration that silently loosens access as a top-severity bug.

Write strong TypeScript, and design tests for correctness, such as differential, property-based, or corpus testing across multiple Postgres versions.

Are comfortable with compiler-style problems: dependency graphs, topological ordering, cycle handling, and diffing.

Can confidently review complex changes in an intricate codebase, including AI-generated PRs.

Have owned software in production with users in the loop: staged rollouts, error monitoring, and triaging real bug reports.

Have led a technical project across several teams, from an ambiguous start to production.

Communicate clearly in writing and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Nice to have, roughly in order of how much they’d help:

Have built or maintained schema diff or migration tooling (such as migra, pg-schema-diff, Atlas, pgroll, sqitch, Prisma Migrate, or Flyway/Liquibase), or worked with pg_dump internals.

Open-source maintainer experience: API contracts, semver, documentation, and community triage.

Familiarity with Postgres extensions such as pg_cron, pgmq, pg_partman, vault, pg_net, pgvector, or PostGIS.

Contributions to Postgres itself, or authorship of a Postgres extension.

Experience with Supabase or another managed Postgres platform.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

#7047. Court approves $5.4M bankruptcy sale of Bacone College assets

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An environmentally focused real estate firm is buying the property and other assets of the Native American-serving institution that shuttered last year.

Along with Bacone’s campus, the sale includes two parcels of land that together total about 67 acres. It also includes a trove of books, archival materials, paintings and other artworks, many of them with significance to Bacone’s history and Native American communities.

ODP and its CEO, Reed Oppenheimer, did not immediately respond to questions Tuesday about the firm’s plans for the Bacone property and other assets.

The real estate firm has made efforts to get certain “significant buildings” of Bacone’s onto the National Historic Registry, according to court testimony recounted in the judge’s order.

The property also has a small cemetery. Oppenheimer must provide “reasonable access to those wishing to visit a grave site,” the judge wrote.

The order approving the sale came over the objection of a former Bacone professor who raised numerous concerns, including that the Muscogee Nation might have a claim to the land under a 19th century treaty. The judge wrote that testimony and documents provided to the court confirmed the legal transfer of land that ultimately came under Bacone’s ownership.

Bacone — originally named Indian University — began teaching classes in a Baptist mission house in 1880.

In the early 20th century, the institution deepened its focus on Native American education, recruiting Native staff and faculty and developing classes in Native American culture. In the 1930s, Bacone established an arts center that developed a distinct aesthetic and expressive movement.

By the 21st century, some faculty members questioned the college’s commitment to Native American students and faculty. In its last years, only a handful of faculty were Native American, and its Native student makeup fluctuated widely, dipping to as low as about one-third in 2018.

In fall 2023, Bacone had just 106 students, less than a tenth of the 1,184 students it enrolled in 2010. By February 2024, The Associated Press reported that the college was teetering on the edge of closure amid lawsuits, debts and the risk of accreditation loss. When visiting the campus, a reporter for the news service found buildings in disrepair, molding Native works of art and accusations that administrators had embezzled funds.

When Bacone initially filed for bankruptcy, leaders intended to keep the college open. But after a federal bankruptcy trustee found evidence of financial mismanagement, a judge ordered the college into Chapter 7 bankruptcy. That kicked off a court-managed liquidation of what was, until its closure, the oldest continuously operating college in Oklahoma.

#7044. How to build an ‘attitude of gratitude’ across grade levels

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Helping students build skills around gratitude can have benefits ranging from daily mindset to overall classroom culture, a UC Berkeley professor says.

Building skills and habits around finding and expressing gratitude can improve students’ day-to-day mindset, their longer-term emotional and mental health, their interpersonal interactions and overall classroom culture.

Educators can help bring this about, although they need to be schooled in how to do so at each developmental stage, according to Vicki Zakrzewski, education director at the Greater Good Science Center at the University of California, Berkeley.

Gratitude is a relational virtue that requires recognizing that someone has done something for you, and that there was a cost to what that person did, Zakrzewski said.

“Gratitude communicates that you really matter,” she said. “Students have to have that ability to recognize that somebody else has done something for you. Gratitude can be fairly complicated when you break it down, cognitively and emotionally.”

For the youngest students, that means teaching what gratitude is in the first place by reflecting on what the emotions surrounding gratefulness feel like, Zakrzewski said.

“You’re helping them define it,” she said. “When is a time somebody did something kind for you? How did that feel? … Students don’t really get a sense of pure gratitude until they have the ability to empathize with others. You’ll see it become more relevant to students who are around 2nd or 3rd grade.”

By middle school, students have the ability to think larger, beyond their own circles, about people who have made a difference in society. When addressing people they know personally, Zakrzewski suggests having them write a gratitude letter.

“That’s one of the most powerful activities,” she said. “If they can, have them deliver it to the person and read it out loud.”

However, educators might have to take a different tack with high school students, who can tend to roll their eyes at a gratitude-related activity. Zakrzewski suggests starting by involving them in a conversation about what gratitude is and how to express it, and then launch an activity so it becomes part of their daily life.

“Of course, there’s the traditional gratitude journal, every day or once a week, where you say what you’re grateful for,” she said. “A powerful thing with high school students in particular is to do self-science: have them notice how they feel before and after they do a gratitude journal."

Students could then decide how often they want to keep a journal, and keep notes about how they feel in week one compared to week four.

Research has shown that when somebody does something for you, it enhances your self-worth and improves relationships, Zakrzewski said. And improved relationships are important in building classroom culture.

Another activity Zakrzewski recommends is transforming complaints into gratitude.

"If you find your students are starting to go down the complaining route, and you want to help change that mindset, take the complaint, but turn it into something you feel grateful for,” she said.

For example, if there’s a math test on Friday, a student might reflect that they are grateful they get to exercise their brain and how that can help them in the future. Cultivating the habit of gratitude and seeing the world through that lens removes a feeling of entitlement, which research has shown provides mental, emotional and physical health benefits for adults, Zakrzewski said.

“That’s a beautiful way of going through the world,” she said. “If you can cultivate that as a student, the benefits are tremendous.”

Research also has shown that not all cultures practice gratitude in the same exact way, and educators should delve into that aspect, as well, Zakrzewski said.

“That’s really important for teachers, if they’re going to do gratitude activities,” she said. “Have conversations with students’ parents, or students themselves, about how gratitude shows up in your culture.”

#7042. Syracuse University adds AI review to undergraduate admissions

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The technology will conduct the first review of applicants’ transcripts, while the final stages will “remain entirely human,” a senior official said.

Syracuse is one of the latest and most prominent examples of colleges turning to AI in the admissions process. The private university will use AI for “synthesizing grades into a uniform scale, gathering information on the student’s high school and drafting a short summary to attach to the application.” The admissions team will continue review all applications.

“To be clear, AI does not admit or deny any student,” Denny Nicholson, Syracuse’s dean of admissions, said in a statement. “Every decision is made by a member of the admissions team, and later stages of review, including admitting the class, remain entirely human.”

Applicant data is stored securely and is not used to train AI models, the university said.

Syracuse expects AI to free up admissions counselors to work directly with applicants and to speed up the decision-making timeline.

“These tools augment the work of our admissions team; they do not automate it,” Jeff Rubin, senior vice president and chief digital officer at Syracuse, said in a statement. “We have tested them carefully, and we will continue to evaluate them to ensure they serve our team and the students we serve.”

Syracuse is facing a projected budget deficit and is reviewing its executive and administrative roles to rightsize its workforce.

While Syracuse did not cite reduced cost as a motivating factor for AI use, higher education and other sectors have turned to automation to trim budgets and conserve resources.

Some colleges that use AI in admissions have also addressed how applicants can use the technology.

In September, the Georgia Institute of Technology announced that it would incorporate AI into its admissions process at several “pain points,” as described by one senior official.

The technology now assists admissions officials in reviewing transcripts and credit transfers, cutting the latter process to “a fraction of the time.”

On its website, Georgia Tech calls AI a potentially “powerful and valuable” tool for applicants and released guidance for its acceptable use.

Prospective students can use AI “to brainstorm, edit, and refine” ideas but “should not copy and paste content” they didn’t create themselves into their applications, Georgia Tech said in a statement.

The institution also said AI can be a “helpful collaborator” for students who don’t have any other sources of assistance in the application process.

The University of Pennsylvania provides guidance on AI for its notoriously selective admissions process.

Applicants can use tools, including AI, to organize their thoughts and check grammar without violating the university’s policies. But “submitting work, ideas, or experiences that aren't authentically” theirs is disqualifying, the Ivy League institution said.

“Here’s a simple test: if you couldn't explain, in an interview or conversation, how you arrived at what you wrote, or speak to it in your own words, it isn't ready to submit,” the university’s guidance said.

On the same webpage, Penn said it uses AI in admissions for “administrative rather than evaluative” processes. They include confirming an application is complete and accurate, “ensuring consistency of the understanding and interpretation of transcript and grading scales,” reviewing transfer credits and assisting with documentation processing. Penn staff members review those results before they affect students’ files, it said.

Like Syracuse, Georgia Tech and Penn said admissions decisions remain in the hands of humans.

But not all colleges are incorporating AI into admissions.

At a conference last month, admissions officials from the University of Michigan, Rice University, and the University of Georgia said their institutions do not use AI to review applications.

Rice’s executive director of admission, Charles Murphy, also expressed concern about trying to use AI to detect AI in students’ applications.

His team enters Rice’s essay prompts into generative AI to get a baseline of what an AI response could look like, he said. But he’s wary of the detection software’s likelihood to flag false positives.

“I’ll speak for myself, not the university. But my thinking is, even if it’s 90% right, every 10 times it’s wrong,” Murphy said. “Is that really good enough?”

#7037. DOL flips independent contractor switch back with new final rule

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Secretary of Labor Keith Sonderling said the agency’s renewed “economic reality” test would provide greater clarity to both workers and employers.

The rule marks a return, of sorts, to the first Trump administration’s 2021 independent contractor final rule. But DOL Wage and Hour Administrator Andrew Rogers said Thursday that the new regulations add “modifications and enhancements” in an effort to create a “streamlined” worker classification analysis.

Those changes include a clarification that the agency’s test applies to the FLSA as well as the Family and Medical Leave Act and the Migrant and Seasonal Agricultural Worker Protection Act, since both use the same definition of “employ” that the FLSA does, per DOL. But the test doesn’t alter assessments under other federal laws, like the Internal Revenue Code or the National Labor Relations Act, Rogers said.

DOL also included 14 separate “fact-specific” examples that illustrate how the rule would be applied to different occupations, such as delivery workers and truck drivers.

The agency named three “less probative” factors that would only be considered in its analysis to the extent that they show whether a given worker is in business for themselves, Rogers said. Those factors include:

Rogers noted that, with respect to any of the factors, the actual practices of the worker and potential employer are more relevant than what is contractually or theoretically possible.

“While courts will have the ultimate say, the final rule and its illustrative examples should aid employers — particularly small employers — in making decisions regarding the proper classification of workers,” James Plunkett, shareholder at Ogletree Deakins, said in an email to HR Dive.

The final rule is effective 60 days from the date of publication in the Federal Register.

#7032. How colleges and schools can grow career-focused dual enrollment

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Career and technical education and dual enrollment are “powerful levers” together, said a researcher at the Community College Research Center.

At DeKalb High School and New Boston High School in northeastern Texas, students in healthcare courses use mannequins to practice caring for patients, while students in welding classes fabricate real parts.

They are participating in dual-enrollment programs developed with Texarkana College that are designed to help high school students make progress toward a postsecondary credential before they graduate.

The community college works with school districts to train high school instructors to teach the college coursework. Texarkana also sends academic coaches to both high schools to help students chart a post-high school plan in their chosen field.

The partnership between the high schools and the community college serves as an example of what an October report from the Community College Research Center calls an "unprecedented opportunity” to do more to pair dual enrollment with career-focused education.

The report estimates that more than 1 million of the nation’s 3.8 million annual high school graduates are taking three or more high school credits of career and technical education, or CTE, but not taking any dual-credit courses.

Sarah Griffin, research associate at CCRC and lead author of the new paper, said that matters because 2025 research from the center found that Texas students with a CTE focus were more likely to enroll in college and complete a degree when they took dual enrollment courses in high school than those who did not.

There was also an economic payoff. By age 24, the prior research found, the CTE students who took dual enrollment classes were earning 16% more than CTE students who did not.

“CTE and dual enrollment together are really powerful levers for students because they are facilitating exploration of careers and college programs for students,” Griffin told Higher Ed Dive.

“Because they're so widespread, they're already serving large numbers of students across the country,” Griffin said. “Therefore, they're really practical for practitioners to be able to leverage.”

Shaun Dougherty, professor of education and policy at Boston College and a former senior adviser at the U.S. Department of Education, said the report's call for a greater focus on career-connected dual enrollment is “fairly aligned with current policy” at the state level.

Dougherty pointed to Colorado's Concurrent Enrollment Programs Act and Kentucky's Dual Credit Scholarship as examples of states providing a favorable policy environment for combining dual enrollment with CTE. The Colorado law covers tuition for dual-enrollment students, while the Kentucky program helps high school students in the state pay for dual credit classes at participating colleges.

“While there has been pushback on four-year college-going as a policy priority, career-aligned programs that span secondary and postsecondary levels and that focus on preparation for relevant skills have gotten far more focus,” said Dougherty.

Griffin said it’s paramount for those looking to establish career-connected CTE programs to develop a vision of the optimal dual enrollment experience for CTE students and how it will help them find their way after high school — whether that means going to a community college, a four-year college or a technical program that’s aligned with a specific job.

Griffin recommended a CCRC-developed framework called Dual Enrollment Equity Pathways for those looking to boost dual enrollment for CTE students.

Pursuing these practices to scale up dual enrollment for CTE students will pay off in the long run, Griffin said. The strategy is a practical approach for helping students earn a postsecondary credential and increasing the likelihood “they'll be able to secure a good-paying job by the age of 30,” she said.

#7023. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

New research suggests that the value of AI should be measured not only by the work it eliminates, but also by the instructional insight and human connection it preserves.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

#7015. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

#7011. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

#7004. AI Native Right Hand of the CEO - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

You dream of a new challenge, of being able to make a company scale and feel that you have a real impact.

If you dream of a life that is more like an adventure than a stable life, then becoming the AI-native right hand of our CEO is for you.

This isn’t another chief of staff or right hand job.

Most companies call themselves AI-first because they gave everyone a ChatGPT licence. We think that’s level 2, and there are 10.

At Paradox our ambition is $10M in revenue with 10 people, each of them running a dozen agents. Here, AI doesn’t assist the work. It runs the loops, in every department. Your job is to make that true for the whole company, side by side with the CEO.

Deep inside you are looking for a new adventure with…

🗽Freedom: working remotely or hybrid, and being trusted to deliver

🔥Ambition: building one of the first truly AI-native companies, with teammates who push AI to its limits every week

😍Impact: changing the trajectory of a whole company, not one line of a roadmap, with a product that transforms people’s lives at scale

Then, you have found the next adventure that will fulfill you.

In a company of fewer than 10 people, the right hand used to be the person who absorbed everything the CEO couldn’t do. Here, the right hand builds the machine that does it.

Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, offers, decisions, procedures, member stories and past experiments, in one shared company brain that every person and every agent reads before acting.

Turn every decision into execution. A meeting, a voice note or a call with the CEO becomes briefs, tickets, follow-ups and deadlines, without anyone retyping it.

Act across every tool. Not a chatbot in a tab: agents read and write in our whole stack, from code to CRM, billing, analytics, ads, email, calendar and documents.

Ship. Produce the analysis, the contract draft, the page, the offer or the process, and put it live.

Measure with real KPIs. Revenue, cash, margin, retention and the transformation of our members, not activity.

Learn and loop on its own. Keep what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and start the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

People set the ambition, the taste and the standards. The machine runs the loop, and gets better every week.

This is already real. Our company brain holds our decisions and procedures. Our growth hub tracks every hypothesis and experiment with its result. Agents cut ads from long-form content, prepare webinars and audit our tracking after every live. And our product is AI-native too: Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day, by text and by voice.

They deserve more than content and more than motivation.

They deserve a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges and grows with them.

Human Intelligence (coaches, peers and mentors)

Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

into one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS, the Operating System for Human Greatness, augmented with AI.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world, and for something that will outlive any individual, its founder included.

Here’s the bet: A small team running a self-learning machine can out-test, out-learn and out-grow teams a hundred times its size.

Reporting directly to the founder and CEO, you will help him run and grow the company.

Your scope is as wide as a CEO/COO’s: strategy, operations, finance, legal, people, product marketing and launches.

The difference is how you do it. In a classic company, the right hand coordinates people and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the work, you design the system, set the bar and make the calls. With fewer than 10 people, there is no department to hand things to. You build it.

❌The old way: a chief of staff takes notes in meetings, chases people by email and builds the weekly report by hand.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: you build the agents that turn every CEO decision into execution. Meetings, voice notes and calls become briefs, tickets and follow-ups. Every Monday, the CEO gets one brief: what moved, what’s stuck, and what needs his call.

You decide what the CEO must see and what he never needs to see again. His time goes to vision, product and members, not to coordination.

Depending on your zone of genius, these are examples of strategic projects you could own:

Growth: run experiments with agents that generate, ship and measure them

Partnerships & sponsorships: find and close partners aligned with our brand, for our YouTube channel and events

International: build the machine that turns our French content into English, and open new countries

Launches: take a new offer or product from idea to live

People: find and assess the rare people who join a team of fewer than 10

❌The old way: each new initiative needs a team, a roadmap and three months.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: one person with the right agents launches an offer, a channel or a country without disrupting the core. That’s what our Pirate Squad means now.

❌The old way: an ops manager processes invoices, chases contracts, onboards people and answers the same support questions every week.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: finance, legal, HR, admin and customer support each get their agents, their context, their KPIs and their loop. You write the skills, plug them into our tools and write the evals that define "good": a reconciliation that matches to the cent, a contract that respects our playbook, an answer a member is happy with.

You give autonomy step by step: first an agent suggests, then it acts once you approve, then it acts alone within a budget and guardrails. An agent moves up a level only when its track record earns it.

Example: in your first month, you build the agent that reconciles payments, refunds and failed charges every night. In month one it only reports. By month three it fixes the simple cases alone, and you only see the exceptions.

❌The old way: someone builds dashboards in a BI tool, exports them to a spreadsheet at the end of the month, and nobody fully trusts the numbers.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: agents watch revenue, cash, acquisition, retention, churn and competitors continuously, find where our sources disagree, and explain what changed and why.

Strategy: screen markets and competition, build growth models, explore business models, partnerships and acquisitions. Then challenge, formalize and ensure good execution of the strategy.

At Paradox things move fast, and our CEO is always reading business books and meeting great entrepreneurs. You’ll turn the best strategies and tactics into systems and agents the whole company uses.

❌The old way: lessons live in people’s heads and leave with them.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: every decision, result and lesson goes back into the company brain, and agents read it before acting. The company never learns the same lesson twice.

You measure the machine itself: the share of work done without a human, the time from decision to execution, the agents that moved up an autonomy level this month, and revenue per person. You push those numbers up.

You help the team become AI-native too: you spot the work still done by hand, and you build the agent or show a teammate how to build it.

And always: our members trust us with their lives. No fake claims, no fake scarcity, data handled with care. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. People do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time building agents, writing evals and making calls, not doing the work by hand. The company’s output grows every month while the headcount doesn’t.

We tell each other things, even if they are uncomfortable. We value growth over ego. At Paradox, expressing disagreement to a manager and knowing how to debate is valued! We encourage critical and strategic thinking.

❌ If you use AI for first drafts but your way of working looks the same as two years ago: wrong role.

❌ If you want to manage a lot of people: we aim for $10M in revenue with 10 people, each managing a dozen agents.

Build one of the first truly AI-native companies from inside the CEO’s office, and write the playbook others will copy. You’ll compete with San Francisco companies.

#7003. ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Planning Huge Rule-Change Next Season to Stop These Celebs From Being Voted Off

Dancing With the Stars has officially wrapped season 35, and the show is already preparing for season 36 next fall. And apparently producers are making a huge change to the voting process to prevent high-scoring couples (think: Jenna Dewan and Val Chmerkovskiy) from being eliminated too early in the competition.

“Producers have already discussed bringing back the judges’ save for next season. Again, it’s still early but that is more than likely going to be brought back,” a source tells The Sun. “They’ve already made it clear they want to shake it up next season, drastically.”

The judges’ save (which was previously in effect from Season 28 to Season 32) lets the judges step in and decide which of the bottom two pairings goes home after fans have already voted. Ultimately, it’s a way to counter fan favoritism overriding high scores.

DWTS abandoned the judges’ save because it ultimately gave the experts more power than viewers at home. As executive producer Deena Katz told TV Line, “I think it should be about America. And at the end of the day, the judges' save sometimes isn't what America wanted. And [eliminations are based on] half the judges and half America voting, and so that's kind of the reason we did it.”

FYI, Dancing With the Stars’ UK version, Strictly Come Dancing, still uses the judges’ save, and judge Shirley Ballas explained to Extra, “I think it's good that the audience choose. They put two in the bottom. That was their choice. And then, at the end of the day, we get to save the dancer that we think is the best. Because sometimes, on your kind of show... they could send all the good dancers home. And then, you know, you're left with a different final. So it's important that there's something for everybody.”

#7000. Getting Ready With Jared McCain

The chronically online NBA player’s routine is all about preserving his mental health. And yes, he has read your comments about his nails.

Jared McCain loves a routine—several routines, actually, which is why there’s a whiteboard in his bathroom, covered with a bulleted list of his daily to-dos (journaling, affirmations, bone broth, and lymphatic jumps, among them). That’s all separate from the 22-year-old’s bedtime and wake-up regimens (silk eye mask, silk pillowcase, silk bonnet, magnesium, and mouth tape, then a multistep skincare process, respectively). And this doesn’t even include many of the things his 7.7 million social media followers have come to love: Jared hanging out in de-puffing eye patches. Jared wandering around wearing Gen Z’s favorite pimple patches. Jared flashing his latest bright-colored manicure.

As he moves through his Oklahoma City home, where he lives with three of his best friends, it’s clear that none of the above is purely performative, as is so often the case with internet celebrities. “I do a lot of skincare because I sweat a lot,” he says as he works his way through what looks like the entire Hailey Bieber–founded Rhode skincare line. “I really like to take care of my face.”

Click through the videos on his TikTok or Instagram page and you’ll likely see that face illuminated by LED lights. “One of my favorite parts about my house is my red light showerhead,” he says. “Red light just creates this vibe and gives me health benefits. It’s number one in my life.” It’s also on his whiteboard three times.

Jared first broke into virality as a starting guard on Duke University’s basketball team. And while his talent on the court would lead him to the NBA—he now plays for the Oklahoma City Thunder—it was something else that captured the wider world’s attention. “I started painting my nails during COVID, and that’s when I also started on TikTok,” he explains. “I played with the polish on and I had a really good game, so I thought, Hold on, this might be the new thing.” (He now has brand partnerships with Sally Hansen and Ulta Beauty.)

“I didn’t really care what other people thought about it,” he says of any negative feedback he got at the time. He now keeps manicure inspiration cataloged on a Pinterest board—“most of the time, I’m feeling colorful; I love bright, bright colors”—and his Oklahoma City–based nail artist Yvie McCrary often sends him ideas to prep for his next appointment.

“In the NBA, there can be a lot of scrutiny, so I try to take care of my mental health,” he adds. In his downstairs “energy room,” he flicks on an LED panel (of course) for his daily yoga flow. “I love finding peace,” he says. “Meditation allows me to assess what’s going on in my life.” It’s yet another habit he uses to work through any unfavorable comments or haters he comes across: “It’s funny at this point. I’ve gotten used to it by now. It’s a blessing to even be talked about.”

Not that everything Jared does is 100 percent zen. When he’s not indulging in self-betterment practices, he’s posting TikTok dances and lip syncs from the Thunder’s locker room, sometimes with special appearances from his teammates.

“Entertaining has always been so fun to me,” he says. “That’s kind of what basketball is, too. Growing up, I thought I was going to be on the Disney Channel or a pop star. I always end up singing some Olivia Dean.” His plea for the singer to bring him onstage during her Art of Loving Tour stop at Madison Square Garden in New York City got nearly 2 million views (sadly, she did not invite him up onstage).

He launches into a rendition of Olivia’s “A Couple Minutes” with surprisingly impressive vocals. “The song could make me cry right now,” he confesses. One of his roommates later jokes, “We need a mute button”—something Jared’s fans have never asked for and likely never will.

Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.
