#6999. Software Engineer - Interfaces Backend at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer to join Supabase’s Interfaces group and help run the backend services behind our developer tools, starting with postgres-meta: the open-source service that runs SQL against customers’ Postgres databases on behalf of our dashboard, our MCP server, AI agents, and other platform tools. You’ll work closely with the engineers who build those tools, and with our Platform and SRE teams.

AI agents are the fastest-growing source of postgres-meta’s traffic, and it sits on the path between developers and their data, so when it struggles, people can’t reach their databases from the dashboard or their agents. The team’s first priority is making it reliable, observable, and fair between tenants at agent scale, and helping redesign it for that scale without breaking the tools that depend on it. Depending on your experience, you’ll own major parts of that work or lead it. Alongside it, the team builds the backend services that give every branch of a project the configuration and secrets it needs.

We want to be upfront: this role carries real operational load. Our on-call process and rotation are new and need a lot of maturing, and you’ll help build them into something sustainable from your first weeks. You’ll be on call for services on the critical path, and incidents come before planned work. Platform teams run the infrastructure underneath; you’ll own the services themselves, from their code to how they behave in production. It’s ideal for someone who enjoys that kind of ownership, thrives in async, fast-paced environments, and stays calm when the root cause turns out to be in someone else’s system.

Help mature the team’s on-call from your first weeks: grow the rotation to cover every service the team runs, write runbooks, and agree when to declare an incident. Then build it, with our embedded SRE, into a sustainable follow-the-sun rotation as the team grows.

Set SLOs with the teams that call the service, and build the observability to hold them: metrics per tenant and per caller, alerts that catch failures wherever they start, and logs you can search across regions.

Make deploys routine with canary and staged rollouts, so dependency and security upgrades stop being risky.

Redesign how the service works with Postgres at scale: reusing connections, limiting or streaming large results, and isolating and rate-limiting tenants, so no single caller, human or agent, can degrade it for everyone else.

Help decide the service’s long-term architecture with our Platform teams, then evolve it without breaking the callers that depend on it.

Write down and agree the service’s contract with its callers, including the dashboard, MCP and AI, and platform workers: how transactions, multi-statement queries, timeouts, and errors behave.

Work through incidents with the networking, Kubernetes, pooler, and control-plane teams when a problem on the path to customers’ databases starts in their systems.

Build backend services for code-first workflows, starting with delivering each branch’s configuration and secrets to the services that need them.

Make small changes directly in the CLI, dashboard, or docs when a capability needs them, with the owning team reviewing.

Review code and designs, mentor other engineers, and grow into leading the team’s technical direction.

Have 5+ years of experience as a software engineer, most of it building and running backend services in production.

Have run a high-traffic, multi-tenant service in production: been on call for it, worked incidents through to resolution, and followed through on postmortems.

Write strong TypeScript, and work confidently in a large Node.js backend codebase.

Understand Postgres from the client side: connection management and pooling, transactions, statement timeouts and cancellation, and large result sets.

Have built observability for a service you owned (metrics, tracing, logs, and alerts tied to SLOs), and used it to debug problems that span proxies, load balancers, or Kubernetes.

Have kept a service healthy under load with patterns such as rate limiting, timeouts, memory limits, and graceful degradation.

Have shipped changes to a live service safely, with canaries, staged rollouts, feature flags, or fast rollback.

Work calmly and constructively with other teams during incidents, including when the root cause is in a system you don’t own.

Communicate clearly in writing, from design docs to postmortems, thrive in async, globally distributed teams, and make progress when the path isn’t fully defined.

Nice to have, roughly in order of how much they’d help:

Have rebuilt or migrated a live, high-traffic service without breaking its callers, using techniques such as shadow traffic, contract tests, or incremental replacement.

Have led the design of reliability work, such as tenant isolation, load shedding, or a staged-deploy pipeline, or set technical direction that other engineers built on.

Have built or operated a database proxy or connection pooler (such as PgBouncer, Supavisor, or pgcat), or an API gateway such as Envoy.

Have secured a service that runs SQL on its callers’ behalf: handling credentials safely, and limiting how much damage any one caller can do.

Production experience in a second backend language, such as Elixir or Go.

Experience with a secrets manager or key management service, such as HashiCorp Vault or AWS KMS.

Familiarity with Supabase, or with how AI agents use databases through tools like MCP.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#6998. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

#6996. Mercury: Product Marketing Manager - Banking & Payments

In 1933, Harry Beck introduced a radically different map of the London Underground. Rather than faithfully depicting the geography of London, he simplified the sprawling transit network into clean lines, clear connections, and a system that anyone could navigate. Distances were distorted, angles were standardized, and unnecessary details disappeared. The result was something far more useful than a geographically accurate map: a way for millions of people to understand a complex system and confidently find their way through it.

Beck understood something fundamental. The best way to explain something complicated isn't to show people everything. It's to understand what matters to them and make that unmistakably clear.

At Mercury, we believe great product marketing works the same way. Banking* and payments can be complex, but the value they create for customers shouldn't be. Our job is to understand what people need, find the essence of what we're building, and help customers see how it can make their lives better.

We're a financial technology company building products that help ambitious businesses do more with their money. From everyday banking and payments to international money movement and emerging capabilities like agentic payments, we're making complex financial workflows feel remarkably simple.

We're looking for a Product Marketing Manager to help bring these products to market, communicate their value, and make sure customers discover and use what we're building.

This role sits at the intersection of Product, Marketing, and Revenue. You'll develop positioning, lead go-to-market strategies, drive successful product launches, and identify opportunities to increase adoption and revenue.

We care deeply about craft, but successful product marketing is about more than a beautiful campaign or a well-executed announcement. It's about understanding customers, building an effective plan, and driving meaningful results.

Atticus makes it easy for any sick or injured American to get life-changing aid. Our mission is to tear down barriers between people in crisis and the aid they deserve. In the last six years, we’ve become the leading platform connecting people with disabilities to government benefits. We also help victims of accidents, misconduct, and violence get compensation from insurance.

So far, we’ve helped hundreds of thousands of people access over $10 billion in life-changing aid —and earned over 20,000 five-star reviews. And we’re just getting started.

In the next two years, we aim to help over 250,000 Americans get access to life-changing benefits. The Lawyer Network (LN) team, led by Erin Casale, is the bridge between Atticus and those firms, and our job is to improve their ability to serve clients and help them grow.

#6992. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

New research suggests that the value of AI should be measured not only by the work it eliminates, but also by the instructional insight and human connection it preserves.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

#6986. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

#6981. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

#6979. Amber Glenn Reveals She Was Drug Tested Ahead of ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Finale

“Third time they’ve tested me this season, by the way.”

Amber Glenn and Pasha Pashkov came in second during an absolutely incredible Dancing With the Stars finale, and their flawless freestyle is even more impressive knowing how little sleep Amber’s running on. The Olympian told Variety that she was drug tested the night before the finale, saying, “I actually had...you saw the drug testing show up last night. Yeah, so didn’t get much sleep. Third time they’ve tested me this season, by the way, guys, what’s happening? So, I’m excited to get some rest.”

At this point you’re probably wondering if Dancing With the Stars is drug testing contestants, which, nope! As Variety notes in their Twitter caption, Amber is referring to being tested by USADA (United States Anti-Doping Agency), which is the “national anti-doping organization (NADO) in the United States for Olympic, Paralympic, Pan American, and Parapan American sport.”

As their website puts it, they’re “charged with managing the anti-doping program, including in-competition and out-of-competition testing, results management processes, drug reference resources, and athlete education for all United States Olympic & Paralympic Committee (USOPC) recognized sport national governing bodies, their athletes, and events.”

Since Amber is currently training for the U.S. Figure Skating Championships (taking place in Utah this January), she’s being tested by the USADA like all athletes. Which might come as a surprise to everyone who didn’t realize Amber’s been juggling training with DWTS!

Her coach, Damon Allen, explained to KRDO “She's been balancing dance on a rigorous schedule with that, as well as, being able to continue training, getting ready for nationals in January. It's been tiring. But she knows how to recover, and she's got a regimen, and she knows how to take care of her body, and figure out how to be at the top of her game at all times, whether it's in the dance arena or back on the ice.”

#6976. Why These Very Unexpected Royals Are at Risk of Having Their Titles Stripped by Prince William

Prince William reportedly wants to “liberate” them.

Prince William has made no secret of his plans to “change” the royal family when he becomes king.

Slimming down the monarchy (no, not with GLP-1s, but rather cutting the number of working royals) and reducing “ridiculous” protocols are just some of the stark ways that the royal family will be deeply different if the Prince of Wales gets his way. But while some of William’s proposed changes seem logical, others have come as a surprise to royal insiders.

Chatting to Radar Online about his new book, Tom Sykes said that friends of William revealed he plans to scrap his children’s titles in the name of “liberation.”

According to the author of Dukes of Hazard: Harry, Andrew and Edward VIII: The Spares Who Shook the Throne, while Prince George will inherit the throne, William is keen for Princess Charlotte and Prince Louis to have other options. In fact, by taking away their HRH titles, William hopes that his kids can pursue careers unrelated to royal life.

“He’s gone and looked under the bonnet and is now making really difficult decisions and changing an organization that hasn’t been changed for a very long time,” another insider told Radar Online.

But if William’s plan goes ahead, it wouldn’t be the first time that members of the royal family went without their HRH titles. Both Princess Anne and Prince Edward opted out of giving their children royal titles, and the former Prince Andrew was shockingly stripped of his just last year.

Previously speaking about the decision to not bestow her children with a fancy title, Prince Edward’s wife, Sophie Wessex, told The Sunday Times, “We try to bring them up with the understanding they are very likely to have to work for a living.”

The Duchess of Edinburgh went on, “Hence, we made the decision not to use HRH titles.”

#6972. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

New research suggests that the value of AI should be measured not only by the work it eliminates, but also by the instructional insight and human connection it preserves.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

#6965. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

#6963. This $120 Dyson Airwrap Dupe Works Even Better on Fine, Thin Hair

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Admittedly, when the Dyson Airwrap came out, I wasn’t entirely convinced it was meant for my hair type. I watched all the reviews: People with long, luscious hair achieve, well, long, luscious hair. My bob haircut (and fine strands) could never get hairstyles like that. Then, I became a beauty editor with access to all the fancy beauty devices, and I finally tried it. Within weeks, it replaced all of my hair tools. I could smooth my ends, give myself a ‘90s blowout, add waves, dry my hair quickly—all with one device. And it’s significantly less damaging too.

But since it was released, a zillion Dyson Airwrap dupes have come out, offering up similar results for a fraction of the price. Because that’s the catch: The Airwrap is great, but it’s a whopping $650 ($500 if you get it on sale right now for Black Friday). My curiosity is piqued every time a new multi-styler launches, but my go-to favorite for the past few years is the Ella Bella 6-in-1 Professional Hot Air Styler. And while it’s 20% off for Black Friday (at the lowest price I’ve seen all year), I’m breaking down exactly why it’s my favorite for fine, short hair.

With six attachments, this all-in-one air styling tool was made to replace virtually every other device in your cabinets. It features a hair dryer, two rotating curling irons (one for each side of your head), and three hair dryer brushes (one oval, one round, and one paddle for straightening). Like the Dyson Airwrap, this device uses hot air, not heated plates, to style, which helps reduce heat damage. The airflow on this thing is pretty damn powerful, reaching 110,000 RPM. And there are three temperature modes and two speed levels to help you tailor your styling to your hair type and needs (typically, the thicker your hair, the higher the temperature should be; and the same goes for speed). The device also boasts a “noise-reduction design,” so it sounds a little quieter than others.

My hair is truly as baby-fine as it comes and dries naturally straight. For this reason, it’s usually very soft and silky (love), but refuses to hold a style for longer than an hour or so (hate). I also color my hair (nope, not a natural redhead), so it’s damaged and prone to breakage. I’m growing out my bob haircut right now, so it’s a little past my clavicle—à la the clavi cut—and I have bangs that always require heat styling to look good. Currently, my go-to styles are typically a smooth blowout that makes my ends look less stringy in the bob grow-out period, or soft waves that add movement and texture. All this to say: The Ella Bella Styler has a lot of work to do (sorry!).

I received an initial rendering of this tool nearly a year ago, and it quickly became my go-to Dyson Airwrap dupe. In fact, I keep it at my desk at the office to style my hair after morning workout classes. The brand just released this slightly upgraded version that I hadn’t used yet, and upon first impression, it looks significantly higher quality. When I opened the carrying case and felt the tool, it’s lightweight (aka easy to hold for a long time if you are working with much more hair than I am) but looks luxe. The packaging is

Totally. At under $150 (a true fraction of the cost of the original Airwrap), the Ella Bella 6-in-1 tool is a pretty solid option if you’re looking to spend less money but still get a high-quality tool. I especially love how it works on my short, fine hair, but I could see someone with long hair falling in love with this too. I’ve already stashed mine in the green room at the office for midday touch-ups, and two people have asked if they could try it.

#6961. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

New research suggests that the value of AI should be measured not only by the work it eliminates, but also by the instructional insight and human connection it preserves.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

#6959. Burgundy Cat-Eye Nails Are the Most Mesmerizing Mani of the Season

There are a few nail colors I consider non-negotiable on my fingertips once fall rolls around: chocolate brown, black cherry, and, of course, burgundy. The latter is a rich, wine-like shade that’s classic, sophisticated, and something I find myself coming back to year after year. For 2026, the color has gotten a seriously mesmerizing makeover in the form of burgundy cat-eye nails. It’s not only a shimmery, dimensional manicure that’ll have you staring at your fingertips all day long, but it’s also a certified trend with searches for the look up more than 300 percent on Google. “Burgundy cat-eye nails have been one of my favorite looks to create behind the nail station,” says celebrity nail artist Erica De Los Santos.

Unlike a traditional glossy burgundy manicure, cat-eye nails use magnetic polish to create a light-catching effect that shifts and moves depending on the angle. Basically, it scratches a really specific itch in your brain that you’ll definitely become obsessed with (if you aren’t already). With the help of De Los Santos, I have all the deets on how to get burgundy cat-eye nails, plus plenty of photo inspo.

Burgundy cat-eye nails are exactly what they sound like: a moody and sophisticated rich, wine-red manicure created with polish that contains magnetic particles. This delivers a shifting shimmer that looks like velvet or polished gemstones—just enough sparkle to make these perfect for the holiday season.

“What makes this look so beautiful is how the depth and color shift change depending on the undertone of the base and the magnetic shade layered over it,” says De Los Santos. “The result is a multidimensional finish that catches the light beautifully, giving the nails a luxurious, almost velvet-like appearance.”

And the best part? There are so many ways to make the look your own. The magnetic effect can be customized depending on how much shimmer you want, and you can work a cat-eye into all types of nail art. “One of my favorite ways to wear burgundy cat-eye nails is as a solid color because the magnetic effect is already so beautiful on its own,” says De Los Santos. “But if you want to have a little fun with it, you can pair it with chrome starburst accents, playful polka dots, or even animal prints. The beauty of a burgundy cat-eye is how versatile it is.”

If you tell your nail artist you want a burgundy cat-eye manicure, they’ll have no problem picking the perfect deep red shade. However, you’ll need to specify whether you want a soft velvet cat-eye finish or a more defined cat-eye look. Bringing a reference photo is always helpful (gotcha covered below), since the placement and intensity of the shimmer can completely change the finished look.

After prep (“I always start by gently pushing back the cuticles, carefully removing excess dead cuticle, and shaping the nails”), De Los Santos applies one coat of a deep plum or burgundy gel polish and lets it cure.

Next, “I apply one coat of magnetic cat-eye gel, preferably in a shade similar to the base color, such as deep red, plum, or purple,” says De Los Santos. “Keeping the shades within the same color family really enhances the depth and makes the magnetic effect pop. Before curing, I use a magnet to create the desired light-reflecting effect.” She flash cures (a short, incomplete cure that’s a few seconds long) each nail as she applies the polish and magnet, and then fully cures all of them at the same time once every nail is painted. From there, leave your cat eye as-is or add chrome powder, designs, or any other nail art you love.

You can also create the look at home with magnetic nail polish and a compatible nail magnet (talk about an easy DIY nail art moment!). De Los Santos explains that gel gives you more time to manipulate the magnetic particles, but she also says there are lots of traditional polish options out there to get the cat-eye effect. With regular polish, prep is exactly the same, but the only thing that’s different is the application. “Apply a coat, immediately use your magnet while the polish is still wet, and move on to the next nail,” says De Los Santos (working with the polish while it’s still wet is key to get the magnetic particles to sit where you’d like them to). “Once you’ve completed all ten nails, you can repeat the process with a second coat if you’d like to build more depth and dimension.” Finish with the appropriate top coat.

Personally, I can’t think of a better manicure for the holiday party season. Check out some inspo pics below.

#6955. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

#6951. Dakota Johnson Wore Cherry Cola Nails, So Now I’m Wearing Cherry Cola Nails

A syrupy upgrade to your typical burgundy mani.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

One of my guilty pleasures is sipping on a large vanilla cherry Coke at the movie theater. I’m really not a big soda drinker, but there’s just something about the combination of a fizzy, cherry-flavored soda, a bucket of popcorn, and a good movie that does it for me. And luckily for me, I’ve recently discovered another way to indulge: cherry cola nails. Yep, my go-to concession stand order is officially making its way to my fingertips, and naturally, I’m eating (or should I say drinking?) it up.

Dakota Johnson was also spotted wearing cherry cola nails earlier this month at a screening of Flesh Impact in Los Angeles, which only further fueled my need to get the look. Similar to syrup nails, these have a glossy finish to them, but are dark, rich, and reddish-brown, just like the classic soda. It’s basically the perfect marriage of two of my favorite fall nail colors, burgundy and chocolate brown. Ahead, everything you need to know about the trend, plus plenty of inspiration for your next nail appointment. Movie theater soda optional... but highly encouraged.

Think of a Cherry Coke: It’s dark brown with touches of red when the light hits just right. And that’s exactly the goal of the manicure version. “Cherry cola nails are the perfect blend of deep burgundy, rich chocolate brown, and a hint of red,” says celebrity nail artist Jacqueline Pham. “Think of that beautiful, dark cherry soda color that looks almost brown in certain lighting but has a cherry red undertone when the light hits.”

They’re also a great way to dress up your nails for the holiday season: “It’s a little sultry, and a fun alternative to your classic burgundy red manicure,” adds Pham. “You can play around with different shades of brown and burgundy and a slight change in either color can create a completely different cherry cola effect.”

Need some ideas of how exactly to incorporate the color onto your fingertips? Firstly, Pham suggests wearing them as a single-color mani—glossy and straightforward with a classic, high shine finish. “It’s timeless, elegant, and looks beautiful on both short and long nails,” she says. She also likes adding a little shimmer too, which you can do with the help of Essie’s Gel Couture Liquid Diamonds in Size Me Up or Color Street’s Nail Strips in Velvet Dahlia. If you’re craving some nail art, you could also do a twist on a classic French manicure with cherry cola tips over a sheer nude base.

First things first, you’ll want to reach for more than one bottle of nail polish. “The secret to achieving the perfect cherry cola shade is layering your colors rather than using just one,” says Pham. “I personally recommend starting with a rich chocolate brown as your first coat, followed by a deep burgundy as your second coat.” This mimics a cherry soda too, with a brown base and a hint of cherry flavor and color. “The brown creates a warm base, while the burgundy adds cherry tint,” explains Pham. After applying a base coat, she says to do “one thin coat of chocolate brown and one thin coat of burgundy, allowing each layer to dry.” After those two coats, she says to “finish with a high gloss top coat to give it that glassy, soda-like shine.”

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered and tested all things nails, from the coolest new polish colors to cuticle care and nail art trends.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6947. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

#6945. Couples Need to Stop Being So Weird About GTA 6

Grand Theft Auto VI came out last Thursday, and somehow the most unbearable thing about it isn't the grown men playing the game—it's the couples.

After the release of the game, my feed became a nonstop slideshow of printed contracts, handmade gift baskets, and girlfriends narrating their boyfriends' disappearance into Vice City like it's a deployment. Couples posted printed agreements that grant the player 10 full days of uninterrupted access.

I'm not here to tell anyone not to play a video game. People should do what makes them happy. But why are we all being so weird about a video game?

Ten days off from the relationship, so a man can play a game. I'd like to meet the woman who has ever been granted a 10-day leave from her partner, her family, her job, and the emotional labor of every relationship in her life. Women don't get to just disappear to pursue their hobbies.

Some women in relationships are putting together “offerings” for their partner. One viral example involved a GTA-themed gift basket stocked with snacks and drinks for the player's sessions. Another woman posted a Target-run basket of her boyfriend's favorite snacks with the caption, "This is your sign to make your man a GTA 6 basket!"

No. It's not. When was the last time he made you a basket for one of your hobbies?

On the flip side, some men are gifting their partners a basket, with the idea that women need something in return—a little distraction to keep busy while he's busy. Women are not toddlers who need to be handed a snack and a coloring book while the grown-ups play. We have friends. We have jobs. We have entire lives that existed before his Rockstar account and will exist after it. We'll be fine. We might even enjoy the quiet!

It all begs the question: Why can't men just have hobbies like normal people? Women have hobbies all the time. We go to pilates, we read, we get absorbed in a show, and nobody drafts a legal document. Let’s keep up that energy. Just play the game and also make time for the rest of your life!

But it isn’t just about people finding the time to play the game. There’s separate discourse among couples about the relationship players maintain with Lucia, the main female character.

Online, some women have expressed feeling jealous of Lucia. Around the game's lead-up, users on TikTok and Instagram were claiming that boyfriends who nurture a virtual relationship with Lucia could be considered cheating. A YouGov poll of 4,000 adults found around 6 percent would consider dating in games like GTA VI to be infidelity. That's a generally small number, but it's still making headway on social media.

Lucia is a character. She is pixels. If your relationship can be undone by a woman who exists only to rob a gas station with your boyfriend, the problem was never Lucia.

To be fair, some women have leaned into the joke. One girlfriend gifted her boyfriend the game with a four-rule counter-contract that ended with the line "Lucia is not your real girlfriend." I respect the bit—but it still icks me out.

On the other hand, there have been people claiming that GTA 6 is teaching men to be better partners. I’d like to politely disagree. The theory goes that watching Jason treat Lucia well, and putting effort into that relationship on screen, will make players better boyfriends in real life.

I'm glad they're being attentive to Lucia. I mean that. It’s a nice change to see the video game encouraging men to be decent human beings (though the bar is low). But at the end of the day, they're buying flowers, fixing things up, and doing the emotional work for a woman who doesn't exist, while their actual girlfriends are across the room eating a basket of Swedish Fish.

Can we all get real for a second? What grown man needs a video game to teach him how to treat a partner? Showing up, communicating, and putting in effort are not secret skills only unlocked by a video game. I promise you your boyfriend is not learning valuable relationship skills playing GTA 6.

So to any couples out there being particularly weird about GTA 6: play the game, enjoy it, and please just be normal. Be a person with a hobby. It really isn't that deep.

#6943. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

#6940. Why Do We All Love a Small-Town Holiday Romance? Guarding Stars Might Have the Answer

The fantasy of falling in love in a small town has surprisingly little to do with the small town itself.

I’ll find any excuse to cozy up with a holiday rom-com, so when Guarding Stars dropped on Netflix, it was a no-brainer. Gossip Girl and Supernatural alumni Leighton Meester and Jared Padalecki are embracing one of my favorite tropes: the good old “small-town holiday romance.” Leighton’s character Hannah goes with Jared’s character Jack to his hometown pretending to be his girlfriend, while actually serving as his bodyguard. It got me thinking: Whether we’re die-hard city dwellers or seasoned travelers, why do we still love the home-for-the-holidays romance trope?

For me, the answer lies in how this specific genre taps into our own deep-rooted desire for escapism. We’ve seen these kinds of fake girlfriend or friends-to-lovers storylines play out a million times before, so their predictability is incredibly comforting to watch. We have, quite literally, seen this film before. And during the heightened chaos of the holiday season, it places viewers in the safe bubble many people crave.

Plus, the small-town setting in Guarding Stars is perfectly cozy. The two head to Jack’s obnoxiously large family home in Montana that’s been pristinely decorated in Nancy Meyers–inspired aesthetics. And much like in one of my favorite holiday movies, The Family Stone, the house itself is just part of the fantasy. The idea of being welcomed into a close-knit community with acceptance might be exactly what we dream of the holidays being like. Being loved by a family that, in the case of Hannah, is not even your own is really intoxicating.

Even the dating looks more fun. Hannah and Jack spend the whole movie dating their way around town, doing cutesy romantic things like attending the local rodeo, getting caught on a kiss cam, and mucking out horses together in the family stables. These wouldn’t be your go-to date locations in a more urban, city-based setting, so their dating experience feels playful and different from everyday life.

And Guarding Stars finds a way to elevate this trope. Hannah isn’t actively looking for a man when all of this unfolds. She’s just doing her job as a bodyguard when she inadvertently falls into a snowball of festive shenanigans. Hannah is doing fine on her own, but there’s something sweet about watching her let her guard down. Guarding Stars gently suggests that by Hannah making herself a little more vulnerable, she’s making room for more people to love and support her. As someone who tends to handle the hard stuff alone (eldest daughter syndrome, anyone?), that really resonated with me.

So often, this genre leans toward women going to the small town and being “fixed” or changed by the relationship they encounter. Their lives in the big city are unsatisfying, actually, and they really would rather work at the Christmas tree farm. That doesn’t happen here. She ends up back in New York City, and that’s where the final action set piece of the movie happens.

Guarding Stars shows that the small-town holiday romance trope can change and evolve without losing its festive magic, because it’s about comfort and connection. We can be cozy and keep our apartments in New York City, and I’m thankful for that this holiday season.

#6938. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

New research suggests that the value of AI should be measured not only by the work it eliminates, but also by the instructional insight and human connection it preserves.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

#6935. A Case for Being Single During the Holidays

valentine’s Day is the official holiday for couples—so why does the fall and winter holiday season feel like a love and relationship fest?

This time of year has literally been deemed “cuffing season”: You’re encouraged to take couples pictures at a pumpkin patch, bring a date to Thanksgiving, drink hot cocoa by the fireplace at Christmas, and have someone special to kiss at midnight on New Year’s Eve.

But even though we’ve been inundated with countless signs that this is the best time to couple up, I’m going to go out on a limb here and say that I actually think there’s no better time than the holiday season to be single.

“The holidays can actually be a great time for creating our own traditions and sense of self,” relationship expert Bethany Nicole tells Cosmopolitan. “Being single over the holidays gives us a much-needed time of rest, reflection, and recalibration.”

So instead of falling victim to the pressures of coupling up during the holidays, why not just lean into being single instead? You might even find yourself enjoying the holidays more. Why? Thought you’d never ask:

According to Newsweek, 65 percent of Americans admitted that they’re stressed about the amount of money they’ll spend during the holidays. And for 73 percent of them, this financial stress actually makes the holidays less enjoyable. Can you guess who people are spending most of this money on? That’s right: their children and their romantic partners.

Sure, you might still have to get your parents, siblings, and your weird aunt a holiday gift—but isn’t it satisfying knowing that you don’t have to budget a huge chunk of change for yet another person in your life? By staying single during the holidays, you’re actually keeping your pockets happier.

The holidays are a time to spend with loved ones. But with so many events centered around the holidays, you might be spending more of that precious time with someone you’re just getting to know instead of with your friends and family. Assuming you like your friends and family, this is not the best ratio!

When you’re single, you can spend the holiday season with whoever you want. You can go to endless friendsgivings without having to drag along a plus-one, you can eat Thanksgiving dinner comfortably with your family without having to check on someone else, and it’s actually really fun to go out on New Year’s Eve with your best friends.

On the other side of the coin, family time in general is not for the weak. I get that, because mine isn’t either. If you were in a relationship, you’d probably be panicking right now about how to explain to your partner that your aunt is really funny if you ignore that she hates your mother or that your dad salutes the turkey before he cuts into it. But when you don’t have a date to Thanksgiving, you can just enjoy the weird and disturbing quirks of your family without having to explain them away to your partner.

When you’re coupled up, you’re expected to be “selfless” and “generous.” Ew. When you’re single, on the other hand, you can just be your normal, selfish self—and I truly think that’s beautiful.

Someone with a partner would probably be expected to give up the last piece of the pumpkin pie or be giving with an extra big piece of turkey. But because you aren’t in a relationship, you can have as much pie as you want—without pretending like you’re not picking up the speed while eating so your partner doesn’t get more bites than you.

Being around your own family for the holidays is torture enough—imagine if you then had to add a partner’s family to the mix? Not just that, but you’d most likely have to pretend to like them, too. And even get to know them!

Meeting an S.O.’s friends and family is a lot of pressure. Even though the holidays are a time of joy and celebration, they’re also super stressful. And you just don’t need the added stress of trying to impress your boyfriend’s dad, who doesn’t believe women should be in leadership positions.

Sure, having a partner around the holidays is nice—doing romantic things with someone you care deeply about can really add to the experience. But if you really want a partner, you have the rest of the new year to try and find someone.

For now, I say save your money, hang out with your friends, and do what the holidays were intended for: eating pie until you’re sick.
